=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a circular array nums and an array queries.
For each query i, you have to find the following:

The minimum distance between the element at index queries[i] and any other index j in the circular array, where nums[j] == nums[queries[i]]. If no such index exists, the answer for that query should be -1.

Return an array answer of the same size as queries, where answer[i] represents the result for query i.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,3,1,4,1,3,2], queries = [0,3,5]
Output: [2,-1,3]
Explanation:

Query 0: The element at queries[0] = 0 is nums[0] = 1. The nearest index with the same value is 2, and the distance between them is 2.
Query 1: The element at queries[1] = 3 is nums[3] = 4. No other index contains 4, so the result is -1.
Query 2: The element at queries[2] = 5 is nums[5] = 3. The nearest index with the same value is 1, and the distance between them is 3 (following the circular path: 5 -> 6 -> 0 -> 1).


Example 2:

Input: nums = [1,2,3,4], queries = [0,1,2,3]
Output: [-1,-1,-1,-1]
Explanation:
Each value in nums is unique, so no index shares the same value as the queried element. This results in -1 for all queries.

 
Constraints:

1 <= queries.length <= nums.length <= 10^5
1 <= nums[i] <= 10^6
0 <= queries[i] < nums.length

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def solveQueries(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Implementation:
from typing import List
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def solveQueries(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(nums)
        pos = {}
        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            pos.setdefault(val, []).append(i)
        
        ans = [-1] * n
        for positions in pos.values():
            m = len(positions)
            if m == 1:
                continue
            for k, p in enumerate(positions):
                prev = positions[(k - 1) % m]
                nxt = positions[(k + 1) % m]
                d1 = abs(p - prev)
                d1 = min(d1, n - d1)
                d2 = abs(p - nxt)
                d2 = min(d2, n - d2)
                ans[p] = min(d1, d2)
        return [ans[q] for q in queries]

Let's decide final code style:
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def solveQueries(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(nums)
        positions = {}
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            positions.setdefault(x, []).append(i)

        ans = [-1] * n

        for occ in positions.values():
            m = len(occ)
            if m == 1:
                continue

            for k, p in enumerate(occ):
                prev = occ[(k - 1) % m]
                nxt = occ[(k + 1) % m]

                d1 = abs(p - prev)
                d1 = min(d1, n - d1)

                d2 = abs(p - nxt)
                d2 = min(d2, n - d2)

                ans[p] = min(d1, d2)

        return [ans[q] for q in queries]
```
This is final.

Actually `dict.setdefault(key, default)` evaluates default argument before call, so `[]` is created each call? Wait, `positions.setdefault(x, [])` evaluates `[]` every time, even if key exists? Yes, the expression `[]` is evaluated before function call, so it creates a new empty list each iteration, which is wasted but not incorrect. For n=1e5, creates 1e5 empty lists, okay but unnecessary. Could use `positions = defaultdict(list)` to avoid. But `defaultdict` is common. Need import collections. Or use:
if x in positions:
    positions[x].append(i)
else:
    positions[x] = [i]

for i, x in enumerate(nums):
    if x not in positions:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def solveQueries(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(nums)
        pos = {}
        for i, v in enumerate(nums):
            if v in pos:
                pos[v].append(i)
            else:
                pos[v] = [i]

        ans = [-1] * n
        for occ in pos.values():
            m = len(occ)
            if m == 1:
                continue
            for k, p in enumerate(occ):
                prev = occ[(k - 1) % m]
                nxt = occ[(k + 1) % m]
                d1 = abs(p - prev)
                d1 = min(d1, n - d1)
                d2 = abs(p - nxt)
                d2 = min(d2, n - d2)
                ans[p] = min(d1, d2)

        return [ans[q] for q in queries]
```
This is clean. The `if v in pos` then `pos[v].append` does two hash lookups for existing; okay. Could use `pos.setdefault` but list creation overhead. Could use `try`:
```
try:
    pos[v].append(i)
except KeyError:
    pos[v] = [i]
```
But exceptions for many unique values could be costly? n=1e5, okay but not necessary. Use `collections.defaultdict`:
```
from collections import defaultdict
pos = defaultdict(list)
...
```

Let's consider if `defaultdict` is necessary? Let's decide final code:
```
from typing import List
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def solveQueries(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(nums)
        positions = defaultdict(list)
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            positions[x].append(i)

        ans = [-1] * n
        for occ in positions.values():
            m = len(occ)
            if m == 1:
                continue
            for k, p in enumerate(occ):
                prev = occ[(k - 1) % m]
                nxt = occ[(k + 1) % m]

                d1 = abs(p - prev)
                d1 = min(d1, n - d1)

                d2 = abs(p - nxt)
                d2 = min(d2, n - d2)

                ans[p] = min(d1, d2)

        return [ans[q] for q in queries]
```
This is final.

Let's ensure code block is exactly:
```python
from typing import List
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def solveQueries(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(nums)
        positions = defaultdict(list)
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            positions[x].append(i)

        ans = [-1] * n

        for occ in positions.values():
            m = len(occ)
            if m == 1:
                continue

            for k, p in enumerate(occ):
                prev = occ[(k - 1) % m]
                nxt = occ[(k + 1) % m]

                d1 = abs(p - prev)
                d1 = min(d1, n - d1)

                d2 = abs(p - nxt)
                d2 = min(d2, n - d2)

                ans[p] = min(d1, d2)

        return [ans[q] for q in queries]
```
This is final.

Now, maybe if there is a value with positions [0, 5] in n=6, distance between 0 and5 is1. Algorithm pos0 prev=5
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def solveQueries(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[int]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(nums)
        positions = defaultdict(list)

        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            positions[x].append(i)

        ans = [-1] * n

        for occ in positions.values():
            m = len(occ)
            if m == 1:
                continue

            for k, p in enumerate(occ):
                prev = occ[(k - 1) % m]
                nxt = occ[(k + 1) % m]

                d1 = abs(p - prev)
                d1 = min(d1, n - d1)

                d2 = abs(p - nxt)
                d2 = min(d2, n - d2)

                ans[p] = min(d1, d2)

        return [ans[q] for q in queries]
```
